_source line 587 · 2026-05-14T08:54:37.557Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_012JV6BeJQiZKWTo9gZx8CTc`</summary>

```text
1	# Goodhart's Law in RL — Literature Survey
2	
3	## Category 1: Reward Overoptimization in RLHF
4	
5	### Core Papers
6	
7	- **Scaling Laws for Reward Model Overoptimization** (Gao, Schulman & Hilton, 2023, ICML)
8	  - Key finding: Gold reward initially increases then decreases as optimization against proxy RM intensifies — direct empirical Goodhart demonstration. The overoptimization relationship follows different functional forms for RL vs. best-of-n, coefficients scale smoothly with RM parameter count. KL penalties increase achievable proxy reward at a given KL but do not improve the gold-reward frontier.
9	  - Relevance: Foundational paper. Our ISR dose-response (β=0.1 helps, β=0.3 hurts) is a precise instance of their inverted-U curve, but in a neurosymbolic verification setting rather than language preference.
10	  - BibTeX key: `gao2023scaling`
11	
12	- **Defining and Characterizing Reward Hacking** (Skalse, Howe, Krasheninnikov & Krueger, 2022, NeurIPS)
13	  - Key finding: First formal definition of reward hacking. Proves that for stochastic policies, non-trivial unhackable proxy-true reward pairs do not exist (unless one is constant). Establishes reward hacking as a near-universal theoretical concern.
14	  - Relevance: Provides the theoretical impossibility result that frames why our ISR hacking is expected, not anomalous.
15	  - BibTeX key: `skalse2022defining`
16	
17	- **Categorizing Variants of Goodhart's Law** (Manheim & Garrabrant, 2019, arXiv)
18	  - Key finding: Four-part taxonomy: (1) Regressional — extreme proxy values overestimate true goal; (2) Extremal — proxy-goal relationship breaks in OOD regions; (3) Causal — non-causal correlations fail under intervention; (4) Adversarial — optimization creates gaming incentives.
19	  - Relevance: Our high-β ISR collapse is best classified as extremal Goodhart (policy moves to OOD region where ISR↑ but PR↓). Provides vocabulary for discussion section.
20	  - BibTeX key: `manheim2019categorizing`
21	
22	- **The Effects of Reward Misspecification: Mapping and Mitigating Misaligned Models** (Pan, Bhatia & Steinhardt, 2022, ICLR)
23	  - Key finding: Across 9 environments, more capable agents exploit misspecifications more. Observes phase transitions — capability thresholds where true reward sharply drops. Proposes anomaly detection as mitigation.
24	  - Relevance: Their "phase transition" pattern matches our β=0.1→0.3 discontinuity. More capable optimization = worse outcome under misspecification.
25	  - BibTeX key: `pan2022effects`
26	
27	- **Confronting Reward Model Overoptimization with Constrained RLHF** (Moskovitz, Singh, Strouse et al., 2024, ICLR Spotlight)
28	  - Key finding: First study of overoptimization in composite reward models. Correlation between component RMs affects overoptimization onset. Proposes constrained RL to prevent exceeding each component's "threshold of usefulness."
29	  - Relevance: Directly applicable — our setup combines exec reward + ISR reward. Their "threshold of usefulness" concept maps to our optimal β.
30	  - BibTeX key: `moskovitz2024confronting`
31	
32	- **Catastrophic Goodhart: Regularizing RLHF with KL Divergence Does Not Mitigate Heavy-Tailed Reward Misspecification** (Kwa, Thomas & Garriga-Alonso, 2024, NeurIPS)
33	  - Key finding: If reward model error is heavy-tailed, policies can achieve arbitrarily high proxy reward with no true utility gain. KL regularization provably fails under heavy-tailed misspecification.
34	  - Relevance: Raises the question of whether our ISR error distribution is heavy-tailed — if so, explains why KL penalty alone cannot prevent collapse at high β.
35	  - BibTeX key: `kwa2024catastrophic`
36	
37	### Survey & Background
38	
39	- **Concrete Problems in AI Safety** (Amodei, Olah, Steinhardt et al., 2016, arXiv)
40	  - Key finding: Identifies reward hacking as one of five practical AI safety problems. Discusses exploitation through environmental complexity and hard-to-formalize concepts.
41	  - Relevance: Classic framing paper; establishes reward hacking as a recognized AI safety concern.
42	  - BibTeX key: `amodei2016concrete`
43	
44	- **Open Problems and Fundamental Limitations of RLHF** (Casper et al., 2023, TMLR)
45	  - Key finding: Comprehensive survey. Identifies reward overoptimization as core RLHF failure mode where proxy-aligned policy regresses on true generalization.
46	  - Relevance: Provides survey context; our work adds a neurosymbolic case study to their taxonomy.
47	  - BibTeX key: `casper2023open`
48	
49	- **Consequences of Misaligned AI** (Zhuang & Hadfield-Menell, 2020, NeurIPS)
50	  - Key finding: When agent's reward covers J < L true features, optimizing proxy reliably loses principal utility. Formal Goodhart demonstration from incomplete objectives.
51	  - Relevance: ISR captures one aspect of formalization quality but not all — incomplete proxy, consistent with their framework.
52	  - BibTeX key: `zhuang2020consequences`
53	
54	- **Reward Tampering Problems and Solutions in RL** (Everitt, Hutter, Kumar & Krakovna, 2021, Synthese)
55	  - Key finding: Distinguishes reward tampering (agent modifies own reward signal) from reward hacking (exploiting proxy misspecification) via causal influence diagrams.
56	  - Relevance: Helps clarify that our phenomenon is proxy misspecification (hacking), not tampering.
57	  - BibTeX key: `everitt2021reward`
58	
59	---
60	
61	## Category 2: Verification-Based Reward Signals
62	
63	### Formal Verification as Reward
64	
65	- **RLSF: Reinforcement Learning via Symbolic Feedback** (Jha et al., 2024, arXiv)
66	  - Key finding: Symbolic reasoning tools (solvers, provers) provide fine-grained, token-level feedback via poly-sized certificates. Outperforms traditional approaches on program synthesis (+31.4%), chemistry (+5.5-33.7%), and Game of 24 (+25%). Smaller RLSF models outperform GPT-4.
67	  - Relevance: Most directly relevant — uses solver/prover certificates as reward signal, same paradigm as our ISR. They show it works; we show when it breaks (Goodhart).
68	  - BibTeX key: `jha2024rlsf`
69	
70	- **FoVer: Generalizable Process Reward Models via Formally Verified Training Data** (Kamoi et al., 2025, arXiv)
71	  - Key finding: Uses Z3 and Isabelle to synthesize PRM training data by annotating step-level error labels. Eliminates human annotation. Competitive results on ProcessBench and 12 reasoning benchmarks.
72	  - Relevance: Uses formal verification tools (including Z3, same family as our solver) to generate training signal. Shows verification-based rewards work — we extend by showing dose-response dynamics.
73	  - BibTeX key: `kamoi2025fover`
74	
75	- **SatLM: Satisfiability-Aided Language Models Using Declarative Prompting** (Ye et al., 2023, NeurIPS)
76	  - Key finding: LLM generates declarative specifications; automated theorem prover derives final answer. Outperforms imperative approaches by 23% on GSM hard subset.
77	  - Relevance: Establishes solver-as-verifier paradigm at inference time. Our work uses it at training time as reward.
78	  - BibTeX key: `ye2023satlm`
79	
80	### Proof Assistants as Reward (Math Reasoning)
81	
82	- **DeepSeek-Prover-V1.5: Harnessing Proof Assistant Feedback for RL and MCTS** (DeepSeek AI, 2024, ICLR 2025)
83	  - Key finding: Uses GRPO with Lean 4 verification as binary reward. Achieves 63.5% on miniF2F.
84	  - Relevance: Same algorithm (GRPO) + formal verification reward, but binary (pass/fail). Our ISR is graded, which creates more Goodhart surface.
85	  - BibTeX key: `deepseekprover2024v15`
86	
87	- **AlphaProof** (Google DeepMind, 2024, Nature 2025)
88	  - Key finding: AlphaZero-inspired RL with Lean proof verification. Silver medal at IMO 2024. Binary correctness guarantee via formal verification.
89	  - Relevance: Demonstrates verification-reward RL at scale. Binary reward avoids the Goodhart problem we identify with graded metrics.
90	  - BibTeX key: `alphaproof2024`
91	
92	- **Let's Verify Step by Step** (Lightman et al., 2023, ICLR 2024)
93	  - Key finding: Process supervision (step-level rewards) significantly outperforms outcome supervision on MATH. Released PRM800K.
94	  - Relevance: Establishes that finer-grained verification signals improve training — our ISR is a form of process-level verification reward.
95	  - BibTeX key: `lightman2024lets`
96	
97	- **Math-Shepherd: Verify and Reinforce LLMs Step-by-step without Human Annotations** (Wang, Li et al., 2023, ACL 2024)
98	  - Key finding: Automatically constructs process supervision data. Used as both verifier and step-by-step PPO reward model. Improved Mistral-7B on GSM8K (77.9→84.1%) and MATH (28.6→33.0%).
99	  - Relevance: Automated verification reward without human labels, similar to our automated ISR computation.
100	  - BibTeX key: `wang2024mathshepherd`
101	
102	### Code Execution as Reward
103	
104	- **CodeRL: Mastering Code Generation through Pretrained Models and Deep RL** (Le et al., 2022, NeurIPS)
105	  - Key finding: Actor-critic framework with unit test execution as reward signal. Token-level value estimation guides fine-tuning.
106	  - Relevance: Execution-based verification reward for code; our ISR is solver-based verification for logical formalization.
107	  - BibTeX key: `le2022coderl`
108	
109	- **RLEF: Grounding Code LLMs in Execution Feedback with Reinforcement Learning** (Gehring et al., 2024, ICML 2025)
110	  - Key finding: End-to-end RL with code execution feedback as reward. Large gains on competitive programming with 8B and 70B models.
111	  - Relevance: Shows verification-based RL scales; raises question of whether Goodhart effects emerge at larger scale.
112	  - BibTeX key: `gehring2024rlef`
113	
114	### GRPO with Verifiable Rewards
115	
116	- **DeepSeek-R1: Incentivizing Reasoning Capability via Reinforcement Learning** (DeepSeek AI, 2025, Nature 2026)
117	  - Key finding: Pure RLVR using GRPO produces emergent reasoning without human-labeled traces. Binary rewards from string matching (math) and unit tests (code).
118	  - Relevance: Demonstrates GRPO + verifiable reward at scale. Binary reward sidesteps the graded-metric Goodhart risk we identify.
119	  - BibTeX key: `deepseekr1_2025`
120	
121	- **DeepSeekMath: Pushing the Limits of Mathematical Reasoning** (Shao et al., 2024, arXiv)
122	  - Key finding: Introduced GRPO — estimates advantages via Monte Carlo rollouts within groups, eliminating the critic model.
123	  - Relevance: The algorithm we use. Understanding GRPO mechanics is key to explaining why ISR reward interacts with it as it does.
124	  - BibTeX key: `shao2024deepseekmath`
125	
126	---
127	
128	## Category 3: Auxiliary Reward Dose-Response
129	
130	### KL/Beta Scaling Laws
131	
132	- **Scaling Laws for Reward Model Overoptimization in Direct Alignment Algorithms** (Rafailov, Chittepu, Park et al., 2024, NeurIPS)
133	  - Key finding: Extends Gao et al. to DPO/IPO/SLiC. Tests seven beta values across 1B-6.9B models. DAAs still overoptimize despite no explicit proxy RM. Smaller models overoptimize sooner.
134	  - Relevance: Direct evidence that beta-controlled dose-response is a general phenomenon, not specific to PPO-style RLHF. Validates studying β as a "dose" parameter.
135	  - BibTeX key: `rafailov2024scaling`
136	
137	- **Correcting the Mythos of KL-Regularization: Chi-Squared Preference Optimization** (Huang et al., 2025, ICLR Spotlight)
138	  - Key finding: KL-regularization is theoretically inadequate for preventing overoptimization. Chi-squared divergence provably alleviates it.
139	  - Relevance: Suggests that even with KL penalty in GRPO, overoptimization (our high-β collapse) is theoretically expected. Alternative divergence measures could help.
140	  - BibTeX key: `huang2025correcting`
141	
142	- **Provably Mitigating Overoptimization in RLHF** (Liu et al., 2024, NeurIPS)
143	  - Key finding: Distributional shift causes overoptimization. SFT loss acts as pessimistic regularizer against spuriously high proxy rewards.
144	  - Relevance: Our high-β ISR collapse may involve distributional shift — policy moves to regions where ISR is high but quality is low.
145	  - BibTeX key: `liu2024provably`
146	
147	### Multi-Objective Reward Weighting
148	
149	- **Multi-Objective Direct Preference Optimization (MODPO)** (Zhou, Li et al., 2024, Findings of ACL)
150	  - Key finding: Extends DPO to multiple alignment objectives. Produces Pareto fronts showing how objective weight ratios control safety-helpfulness trade-off.
151	  - Relevance: Our exec+ISR is a multi-objective problem. MODPO's Pareto analysis framework could formalize our β sweep results.
152	  - BibTeX key: `zhou2024modpo`
153	
154	- **ArmoRM: Interpretable Preferences via Multi-Objective Reward Modeling and MoE** (Wang, Xiong et al., 2024, Findings of EMNLP)
155	  - Key finding: MoE gating auto-selects reward objective weights per context. Fixed uniform weights are suboptimal.
156	  - Relevance: Suggests per-instance β (rather than fixed β) could avoid Goodhart effects.
157	  - BibTeX key: `wang2024armorm`
158	
159	- **Reward Model Ensembles Help Mitigate Overoptimization** (Coste et al., 2023, NeurIPS Workshop)
160	  - Key finding: Worst-case optimization across RM ensembles practically eliminates overoptimization for BoN and reduces it for PPO.
161	  - Relevance: Ensemble approach as mitigation; could apply to our ISR (multiple solver metrics instead of one).
162	  - BibTeX key: `coste2023reward`
163	
164	### Adaptive Auxiliary Reward Weighting
165	
166	- **Learning to Utilize Shaping Rewards (BiPaRS)** (Hu et al., 2020, NeurIPS)
167	  - Key finding: Bi-level optimization learns parameterized shaping reward weights. Can even transform harmful shaping rewards into beneficial ones by learning negative weights.
168	  - Relevance: Directly addresses our problem — when auxiliary reward (ISR) weight is wrong, automated weight learning could find optimal β.
169	  - BibTeX key: `hu2020bipars`
170	
171	- **Reward Training Wheels: Adaptive Auxiliary Rewards for Robotics RL** (Wang, Xu et al., 2025, IROS)
172	  - Key finding: Teacher dynamically adjusts auxiliary reward weights based on student's evolving capabilities. Optimal auxiliary weight is stage-dependent.
173	  - Relevance: Suggests β should decrease during training as the policy improves — matches intuition that ISR is useful early but harmful when over-optimized.
174	  - BibTeX key: `wang2025reward`
175	
176	- **Automatic Successive RL with Multiple Auxiliary Rewards (ASR)** (Fu, Zhan et al., 2019, IJCAI)
177	  - Key finding: Fixed linear scalarization is suboptimal; ASR dynamically selects which auxiliary reward to emphasize at each training stage.
178	  - Relevance: Supports the idea that a fixed β throughout training is inherently limited.
179	  - BibTeX key: `fu2019automatic`
180	
181	- **Adaptive Reward Scaling for Multi-Task RL (ARS)** (Cho, Park, Kim & Sung, 2025, ICML)
182	  - Key finding: History-based reward scaling ensures balanced reward distributions across tasks. Unbalanced magnitudes cause catastrophic interference.
183	  - Relevance: Our ISR at high β may dominate the exec reward signal, causing the catastrophic PR collapse we observe.
184	  - BibTeX key: `cho2025ars`
185	
186	- **UNREAL: RL with Unsupervised Auxiliary Tasks** (Jaderberg et al., 2017, ICLR)
187	  - Key finding: Foundational auxiliary-task paper. Auxiliary task weights (λ) are domain-dependent; different ranges needed for different environments.
188	  - Relevance: Classic reference establishing that auxiliary reward weight tuning is critical and domain-specific.
189	  - BibTeX key: `jaderberg2017unreal`
190	
191	### GRPO-Specific
192	
193	- **DAPO: An Open-Source LLM Reinforcement Learning System at Scale** (Yu et al., ByteDance, 2025, arXiv)
194	  - Key finding: Four techniques for stable multi-component reward: Clip-Higher, Dynamic Sampling, token-level policy gradient, Overlong Reward Shaping with soft punishment.
195	  - Relevance: GRPO variant with multiple reward components; their techniques address stability issues relevant to our multi-reward setup.
196	  - BibTeX key: `yu2025dapo`
197	
198	---
199	
200	## Positioning Summary
201	
202	### Novelty (what we contribute that prior work doesn't cover)
203	
204	1. **First Goodhart characterization in neurosymbolic verification rewards**: Prior work studies reward hacking in language preference RMs (Gao et al.), game environments (Pan et al.), or code execution (CodeRL). No one has studied Goodhart effects when a logical solver's output is used as RL reward signal.
205	
206	2. **Dose-response curve for verification-based auxiliary reward**: We provide the first empirical β sweep showing inverted-U behavior specifically for a solver-based metric (ISR). Prior dose-response studies are in RLHF preference settings (Rafailov et al.) or robotics (Wang et al.), not formal verification.
207	
208	3. **Qualitative phase transition**: Our β=0.1→0.3 transition shows a sharp qualitative shift (PR 81%→18%), not a smooth degradation. This connects to Pan et al.'s phase transitions but in a neurosymbolic context.
209	
210	4. **Graded vs. binary verification rewards**: We implicitly show that graded metrics (ISR) are more Goodhart-susceptible than binary verification (pass/fail used by DeepSeek-Prover, AlphaProof, DeepSeek-R1). This distinction hasn't been articulated in prior literature.
211	
212	### Citable Support (what prior work validates)
213	
214	- **Inverted-U is a known pattern**: Gao et al. 2023, Rafailov et al. 2024 establish this for RLHF; we confirm it extends to solver-based rewards.
215	- **Proxy hacking is theoretically inevitable**: Skalse et al. 2022 prove no non-trivial unhackable proxy exists; our ISR hacking is a predicted consequence.
216	- **Goodhart taxonomy applies**: Manheim & Garrabrant 2019 — our case is extremal/causal Goodhart.
217	- **Verification rewards work at low dose**: Lightman et al. 2024, RLSF 2024, Math-Shepherd 2024 show verification signals improve training; consistent with our β=0.1 result.
218	- **Composite rewards need careful balancing**: Moskovitz et al. 2024 show correlation between reward components affects overoptimization onset.
219	
220	### Anticipated Reviewer Questions
221	
222	1. **"Why not use binary ISR instead of graded?"** — Binary (solved/unsolved) would likely avoid Goodhart but lose the richer training signal. This is an explicit trade-off we should discuss citing DeepSeek-Prover/AlphaProof (binary works) vs. Lightman/Math-Shepherd (graded is richer).
223	
224	2. **"How does this relate to Gao et al. scaling laws?"** — Our β sweep is analogous to their KL sweep. We should plot our results in their framework (gold reward vs. proxy optimization intensity).
225	
226	3. **"Why not use adaptive β?"** — BiPaRS, ASR, ARS show adaptive weighting outperforms fixed. We should acknowledge this as future work and cite these papers.
227	
228	4. **"Is this just reward shaping gone wrong?"** — Partially, but it's specifically Goodhart (measure→target→ceases to be good measure), not generic reward shaping failure. Manheim & Garrabrant's taxonomy helps distinguish.
229	
230	5. **"What's the Goodhart mechanism — regressional, extremal, causal, or adversarial?"** — Likely extremal (policy finds OOD region where ISR↑ but quality↓) with possible causal component (ISR correlates with quality in training distribution but the correlation breaks under optimization pressure).
231	
```

SFT loss acts as pessimistic regularizer against spuriously high proxy rewards.\n  - Relevance: Our high-β ISR collapse may involve distributional shift — policy moves to regions where ISR is high but quality is low.\n  - BibTeX key: `liu2024provably`\n\n### Multi-Objective Reward Weighting\n\n- **Multi-Objective Direct Preference Optimization (MODPO)** (Zhou, Li et al., 2024, Findings of ACL)\n  - Key finding: Extends DPO to multiple alignment objectives. Produces Pareto fronts showing how objective weight ratios control safety-helpfulness trade-off.\n  - Relevance: Our exec+ISR is a multi-objective problem. MODPO's Pareto analysis framework could formalize our β sweep results.\n  - BibTeX key: `zhou2024modpo`\n\n- **ArmoRM: Interpretable Preferences via Multi-Objective Reward Modeling and MoE** (Wang, Xiong et al., 2024, Findings of EMNLP)\n  - Key finding: MoE gating auto-selects reward objective weights per context. Fixed uniform weights are suboptimal.\n  - Relevance: Suggests per-instance β (rather than fixed β) could avoid Goodhart effects.\n  - BibTeX key: `wang2024armorm`\n\n- **Reward Model Ensembles Help Mitigate Overoptimization** (Coste et al., 2023, NeurIPS Workshop)\n  - Key finding: Worst-case optimization across RM ensembles practically eliminates overoptimization for BoN and reduces it for PPO.\n  - Relevance: Ensemble approach as mitigation; could apply to our ISR (multiple solver metrics instead of one).\n  - BibTeX key: `coste2023reward`\n\n### Adaptive Auxiliary Reward Weighting\n\n- **Learning to Utilize Shaping Rewards (BiPaRS)** (Hu et al., 2020, NeurIPS)\n  - Key finding: Bi-level optimization learns parameterized shaping reward weights. Can even transform harmful shaping rewards into beneficial ones by learning negative weights.\n  - Relevance: Directly addresses our problem — when auxiliary reward (ISR) weight is wrong, automated weight learning could find optimal β.\n  - BibTeX key: `hu2020bipars`\n\n- **Reward Training Wheels: Adaptive Auxiliary Rewards for Robotics RL** (Wang, Xu et al., 2025, IROS)\n  - Key finding: Teacher dynamically adjusts auxiliary reward weights based on student's evolving capabilities. Optimal auxiliary weight is stage-dependent.\n  - Relevance: Suggests β should decrease during training as the policy improves — matches intuition that ISR is useful early but harmful when over-optimized.\n  - BibTeX key: `wang2025reward`\n\n- **Automatic Successive RL with Multiple Auxiliary Rewards (ASR)** (Fu, Zhan et al., 2019, IJCAI)\n  - Key finding: Fixed linear scalarization is suboptimal; ASR dynamically selects which auxiliary reward to emphasize at each training stage.\n  - Relevance: Supports the idea that a fixed β throughout training is inherently limited.\n  - BibTeX key: `fu2019automatic`\n\n- **Adaptive Reward Scaling for Multi-Task RL (ARS)** (Cho, Park, Kim & Sung, 2025, ICML)\n  - Key finding: History-based reward scaling ensures balanced reward distributions across tasks. Unbalanced magnitudes cause catastrophic interference.\n  - Relevance: Our ISR at high β may dominate the exec reward signal, causing the catastrophic PR collapse we observe.\n  - BibTeX key: `cho2025ars`\n\n- **UNREAL: RL with Unsupervised Auxiliary Tasks** (Jaderberg et al., 2017, ICLR)\n  - Key finding: Foundational auxiliary-task paper. Auxiliary task weights (λ) are domain-dependent; different ranges needed for different environments.\n  - Relevance: Classic reference establishing that auxiliary reward weight tuning is critical and domain-specific.\n  - BibTeX key: `jaderberg2017unreal`\n\n### GRPO-Specific\n\n- **DAPO: An Open-Source LLM Reinforcement Learning System at Scale** (Yu et al., ByteDance, 2025, arXiv)\n  - Key finding: Four techniques for stable multi-component reward: Clip-Higher, Dynamic Sampling, token-level policy gradient, Overlong Reward Shaping with soft punishment.\n  - Relevance: GRPO variant with multiple reward components; their techniques address stability issues relevant to our multi-reward setup.\n  - BibTeX key: `yu2025dapo`\n\n---\n\n## Positioning Summary\n\n### Novelty (what we contribute that prior work doesn't cover)\n\n1. **First Goodhart characterization in neurosymbolic verification rewards**: Prior work studies reward hacking in language preference RMs (Gao et al.), game environments (Pan et al.), or code execution (CodeRL). No one has studied Goodhart effects when a logical solver's output is used as RL reward signal.\n\n2. **Dose-response curve for verification-based auxiliary reward**: We provide the first empirical β sweep showing inverted-U behavior specifically for a solver-based metric (ISR). Prior dose-response studies are in RLHF preference settings (Rafailov et al.) or robotics (Wang et al.), not formal verification.\n\n3. **Qualitative phase transition**: Our β=0.1→0.3 transition shows a sharp qualitative shift (PR 81%→18%), not a smooth degradation. This connects to Pan et al.'s phase transitions but in a neurosymbolic context.\n\n4. **Graded vs. binary verification rewards**: We implicitly show that graded metrics (ISR) are more Goodhart-susceptible than binary verification (pass/fail used by DeepSeek-Prover, AlphaProof, DeepSeek-R1). This distinction hasn't been articulated in prior literature.\n\n### Citable Support (what prior work validates)\n\n- **Inverted-U is a known pattern**: Gao et al. 2023, Rafailov et al. 2024 establish this for RLHF; we confirm it extends to solver-based rewards.\n- **Proxy hacking is theoretically inevitable**: Skalse et al. 2022 prove no non-trivial unhackable proxy exists; our ISR hacking is a predicted consequence.\n- **Goodhart taxonomy applies**: Manheim & Garrabrant 2019 — our case is extremal/causal Goodhart.\n- **Verification rewards work at low dose**: Lightman et al. 2024, RLSF 2024, Math-Shepherd 2024 show verification signals improve training; consistent with our β=0.1 result.\n- **Composite rewards need careful balancing**: Moskovitz et al. 2024 show correlation between reward components affects overoptimization onset.\n\n### Anticipated Reviewer Questions\n\n1. **\"Why not use binary ISR instead of graded?\"** — Binary (solved/unsolved) would likely avoid Goodhart but lose the richer training signal. This is an explicit trade-off we should discuss citing DeepSeek-Prover/AlphaProof (binary works) vs. Lightman/Math-Shepherd (graded is richer).\n\n2. **\"How does this relate to Gao et al. scaling laws?\"** — Our β sweep is analogous to their KL sweep. We should plot our results in their framework (gold reward vs. proxy optimization intensity).\n\n3. **\"Why not use adaptive β?\"** — BiPaRS, ASR, ARS show adaptive weighting outperforms fixed. We should acknowledge this as future work and cite these papers.\n\n4. **\"Is this just reward shaping gone wrong?\"** — Partially, but it's specifically Goodhart (measure→target→ceases to be good measure), not generic reward shaping failure. Manheim & Garrabrant's taxonomy helps distinguish.\n\n5. **\"What's the Goodhart mechanism — regressional, extremal, causal, or adversarial?\"** — Likely extremal (policy finds OOD region where ISR↑ but quality↓) with possible causal component (ISR correlates with quality in training distribution but the correlation breaks under optimization pressure).\n",

482	  - id: gao-reward-overoptimization
483	    title: "Scaling Laws for Reward Model Overoptimization"
484	    authors: "Leo Gao, John Schulman, Jacob Hilton"
485	    year: 2023
486	    venue: "ICML 2023 (arXiv 2210.10760)"
487	    category: reward-hacking
488	    role: theoretical-foundation
489	    relevance: >
490	      Foundational work quantifying Goodhart's effect: proxy reward rises
491	      but gold reward first rises then falls. ISR is a proxy reward that
492	      exhibits exactly this pattern — ISR can increase while prove_rate
493	      decreases, making it suitable for post-hoc diagnosis but harmful
494	      as training signal.
495	    key_finding: >
496	      Gold/proxy reward divergence follows predictable scaling laws.
497	      Proxy reward optimization past critical point degrades gold reward,
498	      with the divergence point scaling with model and dataset size.
499	
500	  - id: karwowski-goodhart-rl
501	    title: "Goodhart's Law in Reinforcement Learning"
502	    authors: "Jacek Karwowski, Oliver Hayman, Xingjian Bai, Klaus Kiendlhofer, Charlie Griffin, Joar Skalse"
503	    year: 2024
504	    venue: "ICLR 2024 (arXiv 2310.09144)"
505	    category: reward-hacking
506	    role: theoretical-foundation
507	    relevance: >
508	      Geometric interpretation of Goodhart's Law in MDPs: optimizing
509	      imperfect proxy past critical point decreases true objective. ISR as
510	      proxy has partial correlation with execution correctness — exceeding
511	      critical optimization point pushes policy toward high-ISR but
512	      low-correctness regions. Explains plan002/plan005 negative results.
513	    key_finding: >
514	      Formalizes Goodhart's Law for RL: any non-trivially correlated proxy
515	      reward creates regions where proxy and true reward diverge, and
516	      policy optimization inevitably enters these regions.

648	  - id: manheim-goodhart-taxonomy
649	    title: "Categorizing Variants of Goodhart's Law"
650	    authors: "David Manheim, Scott Garrabrant"
651	    year: 2019
652	    venue: "arXiv 1803.04585"
653	    category: goodhart-overoptimization

655	    relevance: >
656	      Four-part Goodhart taxonomy: regressional, extremal, causal, adversarial.
657	      STA's high-β ISR collapse is best classified as extremal Goodhart
658	      (policy moves to OOD region where ISR↑ but prove_rate↓). Provides
659	      vocabulary for discussion section.

662	      variant occurs when proxy-goal relationship breaks in out-of-distribution
663	      regions reached by strong optimization.

669	    venue: "ICLR 2022"

682	    title: "Catastrophic Goodhart: Regularizing RLHF with KL Divergence Does Not Mitigate Heavy-Tailed Reward Misspecification"
683	    authors: "Tat-Thang Kwa, Rachel Thomas, Adrià Garriga-Alonso"
684	    year: 2024
685	    venue: "NeurIPS 2024"
686	    category: goodhart-overoptimization
687	    role: theoretical-foundation
688	    relevance: >
689	      If reward model error is heavy-tailed, policies achieve arbitrarily
690	      high proxy reward with no true utility gain. KL regularization provably
691	      fails. Explains why KL penalty in GRPO cannot prevent ISR collapse
692	      at high β if ISR error distribution is heavy-tailed.
693	    key_finding: >
694	      KL-regularized RL provably fails to prevent overoptimization under
695	      heavy-tailed reward misspecification. Policies can achieve arbitrarily
696	      high proxy reward with zero true utility.

699	    title: "Scaling Laws for Reward Model Overoptimization in Direct Alignment Algorithms"
700	    authors: "Rafael Rafailov, Yaswanth Chittepu, Ryan Park, Harshit Sikchi, Joey Hejna, W. Bradley Knox, Chelsea Finn, Scott Niekum"
701	    year: 2024

712	      predictable scaling laws. Smaller models overoptimize sooner.

    "type": "text",
    "file": {
      "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_3/projects/solver-trace-ablation/literature_map.yaml",
      "content": "# Literature Map for Solver Trace Ablation (STA)\n# Categories:\n#   1. neurosymbolic-rl: Neurosymbolic reasoning + RL/GRPO\n#   2. reward-shaping: Reward shaping for code/formal verification\n#   3. mutation-testing: Mutation testing in software engineering\n#   4. intensional-analysis: Intensional program analysis\n#   5. reward-hacking: Reward hacking / overoptimization\n#   6. fol-formalization: FOL/Prolog formalization with LLMs\n#   7. goodhart-overoptimization: Goodhart's Law theory and dose-response dynamics\n#   8. verification-reward: Solver/prover as RL reward signal\n\npapers:\n\n  # ============================================================\n  # Category 1: Neurosymbolic reasoning + RL/GRPO\n  # ============================================================\n\n  - id: deepseek-r1\n    title: \"DeepSeek-R1: Incentivizing Reasoning Capability in LLMs via Reinforcement Learning\"\n    authors: \"DeepSeek-AI\"\n    year: 2025\n    venue: \"arXiv 2501.12948 / Nature 2025\"\n    category: neurosymbolic-rl\n    role: method-component\n    relevance: >\n      Demonstrates that pure RL (GRPO) can incentivize reasoning in LLMs without SFT.\n      STA builds on the same GRPO framework but replaces binary outcome reward with\n      intensional structural reward for neurosymbolic formalization.\n    key_finding: >\n      DeepSeek-R1-Zero trained via GRPO alone achieves 71% on AIME 2024,\n      showing emergent chain-of-thought reasoning from RL alone.\n\n  - id: deepseekmath\n    title: \"DeepSeekMath: Pushing the Limits of Mathematical Reasoning in Open Language Models\"\n    authors: \"Zhihong Shao, Peiyi Wang, Qihao Zhu, Runxin Xu, et al.\"\n    year: 2024\n    venue: \"arXiv 2402.03300\"\n    category: neurosymbolic-rl\n    role: method-component\n    relevance: >\n      Introduces GRPO (Group Relative Policy Optimization), the RL algorithm\n      used in STA training. GRPO eliminates the critic model by estimating\n      baselines from group scores.\n    key_finding: >\n      GRPO matches PPO performance while significantly reducing training\n      resources; improves GSM8K 82.9->88.2% and MATH 46.8->51.7%.\n\n  - id: logic-rl\n    title: \"Logic-RL: Unleashing LLM Reasoning with Rule-Based Reinforcement Learning\"\n    authors: \"Xian et al.\"\n    year: 2025\n    venue: \"arXiv 2502.14768\"\n    category: neurosymbolic-rl\n    role: direct-competitor\n    relevance: >\n      Uses rule-based RL (REINFORCE++) for LLM logical reasoning on synthetic\n      logic puzzles. Closest competitor to STA's approach of RL for logical\n      formalization, though Logic-RL uses extensional (answer-correctness)\n      rewards rather than STA's intensional structural rewards.\n    key_finding: >\n      REINFORCE++ outperforms GRPO on logic puzzles with superior stability;\n      rule-based reward design is critical for RL-based logical reasoning.\n\n  - id: mellgren-prolog-grpo\n    title: \"Training Language Models to Use Prolog as a Tool\"\n    authors: \"Niklas Mellgren\"\n    year: 2024\n    venue: \"arXiv 2512.07407\"\n    category: neurosymbolic-rl\n    role: direct-competitor\n    relevance: >\n      Uses GRPO to train a 3B LLM to generate Prolog code for reasoning,\n      directly comparable to STA's approach of GRPO-training a formalizer.\n      Key difference: uses pass/fail reward vs STA's structural ISR.\n    key_finding: >\n      GRPO is effective for teaching small LLMs to use Prolog as a tool\n      for logical and mathematical reasoning.\n\n  - id: satlm\n    title: \"SatLM: Satisfiability-Aided Language Models Using Declarative Prompting\"\n    authors: \"Xi Ye et al.\"\n    year: 2023\n    venue: \"NeurIPS 2023\"\n    category: neurosymbolic-rl\n    role: background\n    relevance: >\n      Neurosymbolic approach where LLM generates declarative specifications\n      verified by SAT solver. Similar pipeline to STA (LLM->formal spec->solver)\n      but uses prompting, not RL training.\n    key_finding: >\n      Declarative formulation outperforms imperative program-aided LMs by 23%\n      on GSM; achieves SoTA on LSAT and BoardgameQA.\n\n  # ============================================================\n  # Category 2: Reward shaping for code/formal verification\n  # ============================================================\n\n  - id: lightman-verify-step\n    title: \"Let's Verify Step by Step\"\n    authors: \"Hunter Lightman, Vineet Kosaraju, Yura Burda, et al.\"\n    year: 2023\n    venue: \"arXiv 2305.20050 / ICLR 2024\"\n    category: reward-shaping\n    role: method-component\n    relevance: >\n      Establishes that process supervision (step-level reward) outperforms\n      outcome supervision (final-answer reward) for reasoning. STA's ISR\n      is analogous: it provides structural process-level signal about which\n      components of a formalization matter, rather than just pass/fail.\n    key_finding: >\n      Process-supervised reward model achieves 78% on MATH, significantly\n      outperforming outcome-supervised models. Released PRM800K dataset.\n\n  - id: reward-shaping-rlhf\n    title: \"Reward Shaping to Mitigate Reward Hacking in RLHF\"\n    authors: \"Jiayi Fu, Xuandong Zhao, Chengyuan Yao, et al.\"\n    year: 2025\n    venue: \"arXiv 2502.18770\"\n    category: reward-shaping\n    role: background\n    relevance: >\n      Studies reward shaping design principles for RLHF: bounded rewards and\n      rapid-then-convergent growth. STA's ISR follows these principles\n      naturally since structural necessity scores are bounded [0,1].\n    key_finding: >\n      Two key reward shaping principles: (1) bounded RL reward, (2) rapid\n      initial growth with gradual convergence. PAR method achieves 5%+\n      win rate improvement on AlpacaEval 2.0.\n\n  - id: rlvr-reasoning\n    title: \"Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards Implicitly Incentivizes Correct Reasoning in Base LLMs\"\n    authors: \"Various\"\n    year: 2025\n    venue: \"arXiv 2506.14245\"\n    category: reward-shaping\n    role: background\n    relevance: >\n      Shows that binary verifiable rewards (pass/fail) can implicitly incentivize\n      reasoning. STA argues that for neurosymbolic formalization, binary reward\n      is insufficient and structural reward is needed to avoid reward hacking.\n    key_finding: >\n      RLVR with binary verification implicitly teaches correct reasoning\n      strategies, not just answer matching.\n\n  - id: rubric-grpo\n    title: \"Rubric-Grounded Reinforcement Learning: Structured Judge Rewards for Generalizable Reasoning\"\n    authors: \"Various\"\n    year: 2025\n    venue: \"arXiv 2605.08061\"\n    category: reward-shaping\n    role: direct-competitor\n    relevance: >\n      Uses structured rubric-based rewards with GRPO, similar in spirit to\n      STA's structured ISR. Key difference: rubric rewards come from LLM\n      judges, while ISR comes from solver-based mutation testing.\n    key_finding: >\n      Structured multi-dimensional reward signals improve GRPO training\n      generalization compared to scalar rewards.\n\n  - id: gao-effective-rl-reward\n    title: \"On Designing Effective RL Reward at Training Time for LLM Reasoning\"\n    authors: \"Jiaxuan Gao, Shusheng Xu, Wenjie Ye, Weilin Liu, Chuyi He, Wei Fu, Zhiyu Mei, Guangju Wang, Yi Wu\"\n    year: 2024\n    venue: \"arXiv 2410.15115\"\n    category: reward-shaping\n    role: background\n    relevance: >\n      Demonstrates that learned process/outcome reward models can hurt RL\n      training, producing worse performance than simple success reward only.\n      Directly explains plan005 NEGATIVE: STA's ISR as auxiliary process\n      reward degrades prove_rate compared to exec-only baseline.\n    key_finding: >\n      PRM/ORM rewards may NOT help or even hurt RL training vs success-only\n      reward. Solution: Clipping and Delta techniques to bound accumulated\n      reward magnitude prevent reward hacking.\n\n  - id: prof-process-outcome\n    title: \"Beyond Correctness: Harmonizing Process and Outcome Rewards through RL Training\"\n    authors: \"Chenlu Ye, Zhou Yu, Ziji Zhang, Hao Chen, Narayanan Sadagopan, Jing Huang, Tong Zhang, Anurag Beniwal\"\n    year: 2025\n    venue: \"arXiv 2509.03403\"\n    category: reward-shaping\n    role: background\n    relevance: >\n      Shows that naively combining PRM+ORM signals leads to performance\n      degradation. PROF consistency filter retains only PRM-ORM consistent\n      samples, achieving 4%+ improvement over blending. Suggests STA should\n      only apply ISR when consistent with execution outcome.\n    key_finding: >\n      Process rewards frequently suffer from inaccuracies and reward hacking\n      susceptibility. PROF filters training data by PRM-ORM consistency,\n      outperforming naive reward blending by 4%+ accuracy.\n\n  - id: moskovitz-constrained-rlhf\n    title: \"Confronting Reward Model Overoptimization with Constrained RLHF\"\n    authors: \"Ted Moskovitz, Aaditya K. Singh, DJ Strouse, Tuomas Sandholm, Ruslan Salakhutdinov, Anca D. Dragan, Stephen McAleer\"\n    year: 2023\n    venue: \"arXiv 2310.04373 / ICLR 2024\"\n    category: reward-shaping\n    role: background\n    relevance: >\n      First study on overoptimization in composite reward models. Shows that\n      fixed-weight linear scalarization of component rewards leads to\n      degradation past a threshold. Proposes constrained RL with dynamic\n      Lagrange multiplier weighting as alternative to fixed ISR weight.\n    key_finding: >\n      Correlation between component RMs significantly affects overoptimization\n      thresholds. Constrained RL with Lagrange multipliers outperforms\n      fixed-weight reward composition.\n\n  - id: skalse-reward-hacking\n    title: \"Defining and Characterizing Reward Hacking\"\n    authors: \"Joar Skalse, Nikolaus H. R. Howe, Dmitrii Krasheninnikov, David Krueger\"\n    year: 2022\n    venue: \"arXiv 2209.13085 / NeurIPS 2022\"\n    category: reward-shaping\n    role: background\n    relevance: >\n      Formal proof that for stochastic policies, two reward functions are\n      unhackable only if one is constant. Theoretically explains why any\n      non-zero ISR weight can decrease prove_rate -- this is a fundamental\n      property of reward composition, not STA-specific.\n    key_finding: >\n      Any non-constant proxy reward can decrease true reward when optimized.\n      Unhackability requires the proxy to be constant, making all non-trivial\n      auxiliary rewards theoretically vulnerable to degrading primary performance.\n\n  - id: progrs-outcome-conditioned\n    title: \"LLM Reasoning with Process Rewards for Outcome-Guided Steps\"\n    authors: \"Mohammad Rezaei, Jens Lehmann, Sahar Vahdati\"\n    year: 2026\n    venue: \"arXiv 2604.02341\"\n    category: reward-shaping\n    role: background\n    relevance: >\n      PRM scores optimized as absolute rewards amplify fluent failure modes.\n      Outcome-conditioned centering shifts incorrect trajectories' PRM scores\n      to zero mean, preserving ranking info without corrupting gradients.\n      Suggests STA should condition ISR on execution outcome.\n    key_finding: >\n      Naive process rewards induce reward hacking by rewarding locally fluent\n      but globally incorrect reasoning. Outcome-conditioned centering resolves\n      the conflict with fewer rollouts than outcome-only baselines.\n\n  - id: qwen-prm-lessons\n    title: \"The Lessons of Developing Process Reward Models in Mathematical Reasoning\"\n    authors: \"Zhenru Zhang et al. (Qwen Team, Alibaba)\"\n    year: 2025\n    venue: \"ACL 2025 Findings (arXiv 2501.07301)\"\n    category: reward-shaping\n    role: supporting-evidence\n    relevance: >\n      MC estimation-synthesized PRM data leads to quality degradation; policy\n      model generates \"answer-correct but reasoning-flawed\" responses, causing\n      process reward / outcome misalignment. Directly parallels ISR problem:\n      structurally correct but semantically wrong proof traces get high ISR\n      but low execution reward.\n    key_finding: >\n      PRM performance depends critically on data quality; MC estimation\n      introduces systematic bias. Answer-correct reasoning-flawed samples\n      corrupt process supervision signal.\n\n  # ============================================================\n  # Category 3: Mutation testing in software engineering\n  # ============================================================\n\n  - id: demillo1978\n    title: \"Hints on Test Data Selection: Help for the Practicing Programmer\"\n    authors: \"Richard A. DeMillo, Richard J. Lipton, Frederick G. Sayward\"\n    year: 1978\n    venue: \"Computer 11(4):34-43\"\n    category: mutation-testing\n    role: background\n    relevance: >\n      Foundational paper introducing mutation testing. STA's component\n      necessity testing directly extends this idea: instead of mutating\n      source code to test test suites, STA mutates logical formulas\n      to test component necessity for solver success.\n    key_finding: >\n      Introduced mutation testing and the coupling effect hypothesis:\n      tests that detect simple faults also detect complex faults.\n\n  - id: offutt-coupling\n    title: \"Investigations of the Software Testing Coupling Effect\"\n    authors: \"A. Jefferson Offutt\"\n    year: 1992\n    venue: \"ACM TOSEM 1(1):5-20\"\n    category: mutation-testing\n    role: background\n    relevance: >\n      Empirically validates the coupling effect hypothesis that underpins\n      mutation testing. STA's single-component ablation is analogous to\n      first-order mutation; the coupling effect suggests this suffices.\n    key_finding: >\n      Test data killing all first-order mutants killed >99% of second-\n      and third-order mutants, validating the coupling effect.\n\n  - id: jia-harman-survey\n    title: \"An Analysis and Survey of the Development of Mutation Testing\"\n    authors: \"Yue Jia, Mark Harman\"\n    year: 2011\n    venue: \"IEEE TSE 37(5):649-678\"\n    category: mutation-testing\n    role: background\n    relevance: >\n      Comprehensive survey of mutation testing covering three decades of\n      development. Provides theoretical and practical foundation for STA's\n      adaptation of mutation testing to logical formulas.\n    key_finding: >\n      Systematic classification of mutation operators, cost reduction\n      techniques, and equivalence problem -- all relevant to STA's design\n      of clause-level ablation operators.\n\n  - id: just-mutants-real-faults\n    title: \"Are Mutants a Valid Substitute for Real Faults in Software Testing?\"\n    authors: \"Rene Just, Darioush Jalali, Laura Inozemtseva, et al.\"\n    year: 2014\n    venue: \"FSE 2014\"\n    category: mutation-testing\n    role: background\n    relevance: >\n      Validates that mutation testing (structural/intensional analysis of\n      program adequacy) correlates with real fault detection. Supports STA's\n      premise that structural analysis of formalization components provides\n      meaningful signal about formalization quality.\n    key_finding: >\n      Mutant detection is statistically correlated with real fault detection,\n      and mutation score is a better predictor of test effectiveness than\n      code coverage.\n\n  - id: llmorpheus\n    title: \"LLMorpheus: Mutation Testing Using Large Language Models\"\n    authors: \"Frank Tip, Jon Bell, et al.\"\n    year: 2025\n    venue: \"IEEE TSE 2025\"\n    category: mutation-testing\n    role: background\n    relevance: >\n      Uses LLMs to generate realistic code mutants beyond traditional\n      mutation operators. Shows mutation testing evolving toward\n      ML-integrated approaches, validating STA's cross-pollination\n      of mutation testing ideas into ML training.\n    key_finding: >\n      LLM-generated mutants simulate real bugs that rule-based operators\n      cannot produce, improving mutation testing realism.\n\n  - id: mutation-guided-meta\n    title: \"Mutation-Guided LLM-based Test Generation at Meta\"\n    authors: \"Christopher Foster et al.\"\n    year: 2025\n    venue: \"FSE 2025\"\n    category: mutation-testing\n    role: background\n    relevance: >\n      Industrial application of mutation-guided LLM test generation at\n      Meta. Demonstrates that mutation analysis can guide LLM behavior\n      in production -- analogous to how STA uses mutation (ablation) to\n      guide GRPO reward signal.\n    key_finding: >\n      Mutation-guided approach generates tests that kill significantly\n      more mutants than unguided LLM test generation at Meta scale.\n\n  - id: mutation-consistency-llm\n    title: \"Mutation-based Consistency Testing for Evaluating the Code Understanding Capability of LLMs\"\n    authors: \"Various\"\n    year: 2024\n    venue: \"arXiv 2401.05940\"\n    category: mutation-testing\n    role: background\n    relevance: >\n      Uses code mutations to evaluate LLM code understanding consistency.\n      Conceptually related to STA: both use systematic code/formula\n      mutations to assess model capabilities.\n    key_finding: >\n      LLMs show significant inconsistency when evaluated on mutated code\n      variants, suggesting superficial pattern matching.\n\n  # ============================================================\n  # Category 4: Intensional program analysis\n  # ============================================================\n\n  - id: deepmutation\n    title: \"A Probabilistic Framework for Mutation Testing in Deep Neural Networks\"\n    authors: \"Various\"\n    year: 2023\n    venue: \"Information and Software Technology 2023\"\n    category: intensional-analysis\n    role: background\n    relevance: >\n      Extends mutation testing to DNN testing with probabilistic mutation\n      operators. Parallel to STA's extension of mutation testing from\n      traditional programs to logical formulas.\n    key_finding: >\n      Probabilistic mutation framework enables meaningful test adequacy\n      assessment for neural networks through structural perturbation.\n\n  # ============================================================\n  # Category 5: Reward hacking / overoptimization\n  # ============================================================\n\n  - id: inform-reward-hacking\n    title: \"InfoRM: Mitigating Reward Hacking in RLHF via Information-Theoretic Reward Modeling\"\n    authors: \"Various\"\n    year: 2024\n    venue: \"NeurIPS 2024\"\n    category: reward-hacking\n    role: background\n    relevance: >\n      Proposes information-bottleneck approach to detect and mitigate reward\n      hacking. STA's ISR addresses the same problem differently: by grounding\n      reward in verifiable structural properties (component necessity), it\n      reduces the attack surface for reward hacking.\n    key_finding: >\n      Variational information bottleneck filters irrelevant features from\n      reward model; Cluster Separation Index detects reward overoptimization.\n\n  - id: mo-grpo\n    title: \"MO-GRPO: Mitigating Reward Hacking of Group Relative Policy Optimization on Multi-Objective Problems\"\n    authors: \"CyberAgent AI Lab\"\n    year: 2025\n    venue: \"arXiv 2509.22047\"\n    category: reward-hacking\n    role: background\n    relevance: >\n      Directly addresses GRPO reward hacking in multi-objective settings.\n      STA's ISR can be viewed as adding a structural objective to the\n      correctness objective, making MO-GRPO's insights directly applicable.\n    key_finding: >\n      Standard GRPO normalizes after summing rewards, allowing high-variance\n      objectives to dominate. MO-GRPO equalizes scales before aggregation.\n\n  - id: reward-hacking-survey\n    title: \"Reward Hacking in the Era of Large Models: Mechanisms, Emergent Misalignment, Challenges\"\n    authors: \"Various\"\n    year: 2026\n    venue: \"arXiv 2604.13602\"\n    category: reward-hacking\n    role: background\n    relevance: >\n      Comprehensive survey of reward hacking mechanisms in the LLM era.\n      Provides theoretical framework for understanding why STA's structural\n      reward is more robust than pure outcome-based reward.\n    key_finding: >\n      Reward hacking manifests through specification gaming, reward model\n      exploitation, and emergent misalignment; structural verification\n      rewards are more robust than learned reward models.\n\n  - id: energy-loss-reward-hacking\n    title: \"The Energy Loss Phenomenon in RLHF: A New Perspective on Mitigating Reward Hacking\"\n    authors: \"Various\"\n    year: 2025\n    venue: \"ICML 2025\"\n    category: reward-hacking\n    role: background\n    relevance: >\n      Identifies energy loss in LLM final layer as an indicator of reward\n      hacking during RL. Provides complementary diagnostic to STA's\n      structural reward approach.\n    key_finding: >\n      Energy loss in the final layer increases during RL and excessive\n      increase characterizes reward hacking; EPPO algorithm penalizes\n      this to mitigate hacking.\n\n  - id: spurious-rewards\n    title: \"Spurious Rewards: Rethinking Training Signals in RLVR\"\n    authors: \"Ruixin Yang et al.\"\n    year: 2025\n    venue: \"arXiv 2506.10947 / NeurIPS 2025 submission\"\n    category: reward-hacking\n    role: direct-evidence\n    relevance: >\n      Most directly relevant to STA findings. GRPO+random reward on\n      Qwen2.5-Math improves 21.4%, nearly matching ground-truth reward's\n      29.1%. Matches our plan017 finding: additive random (PR=48%) >\n      additive ISR (PR=18%). Structured metrics can be worse than random\n      noise due to systematic bias vs pure variance.\n    key_finding: >\n      Random reward nearly matches ground-truth reward in GRPO training,\n      suggesting GRPO's group normalization extracts signal from noise.\n      Effect is model-dependent (works on Qwen2.5, not Llama3).\n\n  - id: gao-reward-overoptimization\n    title: \"Scaling Laws for Reward Model Overoptimization\"\n    authors: \"Leo Gao, John Schulman, Jacob Hilton\"\n    year: 2023\n    venue: \"ICML 2023 (arXiv 2210.10760)\"\n    category: reward-hacking\n    role: theoretical-foundation\n    relevance: >\n      Foundational work quantifying Goodhart's effect: proxy reward rises\n      but gold reward first rises then falls. ISR is a proxy reward that\n      exhibits exactly this pattern — ISR can increase while prove_rate\n      decreases, making it suitable for post-hoc diagnosis but harmful\n      as training signal.\n    key_finding: >\n      Gold/proxy reward divergence follows predictable scaling laws.\n      Proxy reward optimization past critical point degrades gold reward,\n      with the divergence point scaling with model and dataset size.\n\n  - id: karwowski-goodhart-rl\n    title: \"Goodhart's Law in Reinforcement Learning\"\n    authors: \"Jacek Karwowski, Oliver Hayman, Xingjian Bai, Klaus Kiendlhofer, Charlie Griffin, Joar Skalse\"\n    year: 2024\n    venue: \"ICLR 2024 (arXiv 2310.09144)\"\n    category: reward-hacking\n    role: theoretical-foundation\n    relevance: >\n      Geometric interpretation of Goodhart's Law in MDPs: optimizing\n      imperfect proxy past critical point decreases true objective. ISR as\n      proxy has partial correlation with execution correctness — exceeding\n      critical optimization point pushes policy toward high-ISR but\n      low-correctness regions. Explains plan002/plan005 negative results.\n    key_finding: >\n      Formalizes Goodhart's Law for RL: any non-trivially correlated proxy\n      reward creates regions where proxy and true reward diverge, and\n      policy optimization inevitably enters these regions.\n\n  # ============================================================\n  # Category 6: FOL/Prolog formalization with LLMs\n  # ============================================================\n\n  - id: folio\n    title: \"FOLIO: Natural Language Reasoning with First-Order Logic\"\n    authors: \"Simeng Han et al.\"\n    year: 2022\n    venue: \"arXiv 2209.00840 / EMNLP 2024\"\n    category: fol-formalization\n    role: evaluation\n    relevance: >\n      Primary FOL reasoning benchmark. STA uses FOLIO-style NL->FOL\n      formalization as the target task for GRPO training. FOLIO's\n      expert-annotated FOL annotations serve as reference formalizations.\n    key_finding: >\n      1,430 NL-FOL paired examples with reasoning depths up to 5+;\n      GPT-4 still struggles on the hardest examples.\n\n  - id: proofwriter\n    title: \"ProofWriter: Generating Implications, Proofs, and Abductive Statements over Natural Language\"\n    authors: \"Oyvind Tafjord, Bhavana Dalvi, Peter Clark\"\n    year: 2021\n    venue: \"Findings of ACL-IJCNLP 2021\"\n    category: fol-formalization\n    role: evaluation\n    relevance: >\n      Source dataset for STA's training data. ProofWriter provides\n      rule-based logical reasoning problems in natural language that\n      STA uses for training formalizer models.\n    key_finding: >\n      Generative model can reliably produce implications and proofs;\n      accuracy exceeds prior methods by 9% and generalizes to unseen\n      proof depths.\n\n  - id: logic-lm\n    title: \"Logic-LM: Empowering Large Language Models with Symbolic Solvers for Faithful Logical Reasoning\"\n    authors: \"Liangming Pan, Alon Albalak, Xinyi Wang, William Wang\"\n    year: 2023\n    venue: \"Findings of EMNLP 2023\"\n    category: fol-formalization\n    role: direct-competitor\n    relevance: >\n      LLM->symbolic solver pipeline for logical reasoning, directly\n      comparable to STA's formalizer->Prolog solver pipeline. Key\n      difference: Logic-LM uses prompting, STA uses GRPO training.\n    key_finding: >\n      LLM+solver achieves 39.2% improvement over LLM-alone on logical\n      reasoning benchmarks including ProofWriter and FOLIO.\n\n  - id: linc\n    title: \"LINC: A Neurosymbolic Approach for Logical Reasoning by Combining Language Models with First-Order Logic Provers\"\n    authors: \"Theo X. Olausson, Alex Gu, Ben Lipkin, et al.\"\n    year: 2023\n    venue: \"EMNLP 2023\"\n    category: fol-formalization\n    role: direct-competitor\n    relevance: >\n      Neurosymbolic approach where LLM translates NL to FOL for theorem\n      prover. Directly comparable to STA's NL->Prolog translation.\n      LINC uses prompting-based approach; STA uses RL training.\n    key_finding: >\n      StarCoder+ (15.5B) with LINC outperforms GPT-3.5 and GPT-4 with\n      CoT by 38% and 10% respectively on ProofWriter.\n\n  - id: fover\n    title: \"FoVer: First-Order Logic Verification for Natural Language Reasoning\"\n    authors: \"Yu Pei, Yongping Du, Xingnan Jin\"\n    year: 2025\n    venue: \"TACL 13:1340-1359\"\n    category: fol-formalization\n    role: direct-competitor\n    relevance: >\n      Uses LLM->FOL->Z3 pipeline for verifying NL reasoning correctness.\n      Similar to STA's verification pipeline but focuses on post-hoc\n      verification rather than training reward signal.\n    key_finding: >\n      FOL verification via Z3 significantly outperforms existing methods\n      on ProofWriter and FOLIO benchmarks.\n\n  - id: lorp\n    title: \"LoRP: LLM-based Logical Reasoning via Prolog\"\n    authors: \"Various\"\n    year: 2025\n    venue: \"Knowledge-Based Systems 2025\"\n    category: fol-formalization\n    role: direct-competitor\n    relevance: >\n      LLM->Prolog pipeline with FOL-to-Prolog translation mechanism.\n      Closest to STA's formalization target (Prolog), though LoRP\n      uses prompting while STA uses GRPO training with structural reward.\n    key_finding: >\n      Systematic FOL-to-Prolog translation extends Prolog expressiveness;\n      achieves SoTA accuracy on logical reasoning with three-vote majority.\n\n  - id: nl2fol\n    title: \"NL2FOL: Translating Natural Language to First-Order Logic for Logical Fallacy Detection\"\n    authors: \"Lalwani, Kim, et al.\"\n    year: 2024\n    venue: \"arXiv 2405.02318\"\n    category: fol-formalization\n    role: background\n    relevance: >\n      Step-by-step NL->FOL translation using LLMs with SMT solver\n      verification. Demonstrates the NL->formal logic pipeline that\n      STA aims to improve via RL training.\n    key_finding: >\n      Neurosymbolic NL2FOL achieves 78% F1 on LOGIC dataset and\n      generalizes to LogicClimate (80% F1), outperforming supervised\n      models by 21%.\n\n  - id: logica2\n    title: \"LogiQA 2.0: An Improved Dataset for Logical Reasoning in Natural Language Understanding\"\n    authors: \"Liu et al.\"\n    year: 2023\n    venue: \"IEEE/ACM TASLP 2023\"\n    category: fol-formalization\n    role: evaluation\n    relevance: >\n      Logical reasoning evaluation benchmark derived from Chinese civil\n      service exams. Provides supplementary evaluation for STA's\n      formalization capabilities.\n    key_finding: >\n      8,678 multiple-choice questions covering categorical, disjunctive,\n      and conditional reasoning; widely used LLM benchmark.\n\n  # ============================================================\n  # Category 7: Goodhart's Law theory and dose-response dynamics\n  # ============================================================\n\n  - id: manheim-goodhart-taxonomy\n    title: \"Categorizing Variants of Goodhart's Law\"\n    authors: \"David Manheim, Scott Garrabrant\"\n    year: 2019\n    venue: \"arXiv 1803.04585\"\n    category: goodhart-overoptimization\n    role: theoretical-foundation\n    relevance: >\n      Four-part Goodhart taxonomy: regressional, extremal, causal, adversarial.\n      STA's high-β ISR collapse is best classified as extremal Goodhart\n      (policy moves to OOD region where ISR↑ but prove_rate↓). Provides\n      vocabulary for discussion section.\n    key_finding: >\n      First systematic categorization of Goodhart failure modes. Extremal\n      variant occurs when proxy-goal relationship breaks in out-of-distribution\n      regions reached by strong optimization.\n\n  - id: pan-reward-misspecification\n    title: \"The Effects of Reward Misspecification: Mapping and Mitigating Misaligned Models\"\n    authors: \"Alexander Pan, Kush Bhatia, Jacob Steinhardt\"\n    year: 2022\n    venue: \"ICLR 2022\"\n    category: goodhart-overoptimization\n    role: theoretical-foundation\n    relevance: >\n      Across 9 environments, more capable agents exploit misspecifications more.\n      Observes phase transitions — capability thresholds where true reward\n      sharply drops. Matches STA's β=0.1→0.3 discontinuity.\n    key_finding: >\n      Reward misspecification causes phase transitions where true reward sharply\n      drops past capability thresholds. More capable optimization produces\n      worse outcomes under misspecification.\n\n  - id: kwa-catastrophic-goodhart\n    title: \"Catastrophic Goodhart: Regularizing RLHF with KL Divergence Does Not Mitigate Heavy-Tailed Reward Misspecification\"\n    authors: \"Tat-Thang Kwa, Rachel Thomas, Adrià Garriga-Alonso\"\n    year: 2024\n    venue: \"NeurIPS 2024\"\n    category: goodhart-overoptimization\n    role: theoretical-foundation\n    relevance: >\n      If reward model error is heavy-tailed, policies achieve arbitrarily\n      high proxy reward with no true utility gain. KL regularization provably\n      fails. Explains why KL penalty in GRPO cannot prevent ISR collapse\n      at high β if ISR error distribution is heavy-tailed.\n    key_finding: >\n      KL-regularized RL provably fails to prevent overoptimization under\n      heavy-tailed reward misspecification. Policies can achieve arbitrarily\n      high proxy reward with zero true utility.\n\n  - id: rafailov-daa-scaling\n    title: \"Scaling Laws for Reward Model Overoptimization in Direct Alignment Algorithms\"\n    authors: \"Rafael Rafailov, Yaswanth Chittepu, Ryan Park, Harshit Sikchi, Joey Hejna, W. Bradley Knox, Chelsea Finn, Scott Niekum\"\n    year: 2024\n    venue: \"NeurIPS 2024\"\n    category: goodhart-overoptimization\n    role: supporting-evidence\n    relevance: >\n      Extends Gao et al. to DPO/IPO/SLiC. Tests seven β values across\n      1B-6.9B models. DAAs still overoptimize despite no explicit proxy RM.\n      Validates β as a \"dose\" parameter — directly analogous to STA's\n      ISR weight sweep.\n    key_finding: >\n      Direct alignment algorithms exhibit overoptimization following\n      predictable scaling laws. Smaller models overoptimize sooner.\n      β-controlled dose-response is a general phenomenon across RL paradigms.\n\n  - id: huang-chi-squared\n    title: \"Correcting the Mythos of KL-Regularization: Direct and Contrastive Distribution Correction\"\n    authors: \"Afra Feyza Akyürek, Banghua Zhu, Sham Kakade, Jiantao Jiao\"\n    year: 2025\n    venue: \"ICLR 2025 Spotlight\"\n    category: goodhart-overoptimization\n    role: supporting-evidence\n    relevance: >\n      KL-regularization is theoretically inadequate for preventing\n      overoptimization. Chi-squared divergence provably alleviates it.\n      Suggests that even with KL penalty in GRPO, STA's high-β collapse\n      is theoretically expected.\n    key_finding: >\n      Chi-squared preference optimization provably mitigates overoptimization\n      where KL-regularization fails. Alternative divergence measures offer\n      stronger guarantees.\n\n  - id: coste-rm-ensembles\n    title: \"Reward Model Ensembles Help Mitigate Overoptimization\"\n    authors: \"Thomas Coste, Usman Anwar, Robert Kirk, David Krueger\"\n    year: 2023\n    venue: \"NeurIPS 2023 Workshop on Alignment\"\n    category: goodhart-overoptimization\n    role: background\n    relevance: >\n      Worst-case optimization across RM ensembles practically eliminates\n      overoptimization for BoN and reduces it for PPO. Suggests ensemble\n      of multiple solver metrics (instead of single ISR) as mitigation.\n    key_finding: >\n      RM ensembles with worst-case aggregation mitigate overoptimization.\n      Ensemble size and diversity directly affect robustness.\n\n  # ============================================================\n  # Category 8: Solver/prover as RL reward signal\n  # ============================================================\n\n  - id: rlsf\n    title: \"Reinforcement Learning via Symbolic Feedback\"\n    authors: \"Abhinav Jha, Shikhar Murty, Zico Kolter, et al.\"\n    year: 2024\n    venue: \"arXiv 2405.16661\"\n    category: verification-reward\n    role: direct-competitor\n    relevance: >\n      Most directly relevant: uses solver/prover certificates as fine-grained\n      token-level reward signal. Outperforms traditional approaches on program\n      synthesis (+31.4%). STA extends this paradigm and shows when it breaks\n      (Goodhart effects at high auxiliary reward weight).\n    key_finding: >\n      Symbolic reasoning tools provide poly-sized certificates as reward.\n      Smaller RLSF models outperform GPT-4 on structured generation tasks.\n\n  - id: fover-prm\n    title: \"FoVer: Generalizable Process Reward Models via Formally Verified Training Data\"\n    authors: \"Ryo Kamoi, Yusen Zhang, Nan Zhang, Jiawei Han, Rui Zhang\"\n    year: 2025\n    venue: \"arXiv 2502.01345\"\n    category: verification-reward\n    role: supporting-evidence\n    relevance: >\n      Uses Z3 and Isabelle to synthesize PRM training data by annotating\n      step-level error labels without human annotation. Same solver family\n      as STA. Shows verification-based rewards work; STA extends by showing\n      dose-response dynamics.\n    key_finding: >\n      Formally verified training data enables generalizable process reward\n      models competitive on ProcessBench and 12 reasoning benchmarks.\n\n  - id: deepseek-prover-v15\n    title: \"DeepSeek-Prover-V1.5: Harnessing Proof Assistant Feedback for Reinforcement Learning and Monte-Carlo Tree Search\"\n    authors: \"DeepSeek AI\"\n    year: 2024\n    venue: \"ICLR 2025 (arXiv 2408.08152)\"\n    category: verification-reward\n    role: method-component\n    relevance: >\n      Uses GRPO with Lean 4 verification as binary reward. Same algorithm\n      (GRPO) + formal verification, but binary (pass/fail). Binary reward\n      avoids the graded-metric Goodhart risk that STA identifies with ISR.\n    key_finding: >\n      GRPO + binary Lean verification achieves 63.5% on miniF2F. Binary\n      correctness guarantee via formal verification sidesteps proxy reward\n      overoptimization.\n\n  - id: alphaproof\n    title: \"AlphaProof\"\n    authors: \"Google DeepMind\"\n    year: 2024\n    venue: \"Nature 2025\"\n    category: verification-reward\n    role: background\n    relevance: >\n      AlphaZero-inspired RL with Lean proof verification at scale.\n      Silver medal at IMO 2024. Binary verification reward avoids the\n      Goodhart problem STA identifies with graded verification metrics.\n    key_finding: >\n      Binary formal verification as RL reward scales to IMO-level\n      mathematical reasoning. Demonstrates verification-reward RL works\n      at the frontier of mathematical difficulty.\n\n  - id: math-shepherd\n    title: \"Math-Shepherd: Verify and Reinforce LLMs Step-by-step without Human Annotations\"\n    authors: \"Peiyi Wang, Lei Li, Zhihong Shao, R.X. Xu, et al.\"\n    year: 2024\n    venue: \"ACL 2024 (arXiv 2312.08935)\"\n    category: verification-reward\n    role: supporting-evidence\n    relevance: >\n      Automatically constructs process supervision data and uses it as\n      step-by-step PPO reward. Automated verification reward without human\n      labels, analogous to STA's automated ISR computation via solver.\n    key_finding: >\n      Automated process supervision improves Mistral-7B on GSM8K\n      (77.9→84.1%) and MATH (28.6→33.0%) without human annotation.\n\n  # ============================================================\n  # Additional reward-shaping papers (Goodhart survey additions)\n  # ============================================================\n\n  - id: bipars\n    title: \"Learning to Utilize Shaping Rewards: A New Approach of Reward Shaping\"\n    authors: \"Yujing Hu, Weixun Wang, Hangtian Jia, et al.\"\n    year: 2020\n    venue: \"NeurIPS 2020\"\n    category: reward-shaping\n    role: background\n    relevance: >\n      Bi-level optimization learns parameterized shaping reward weights.\n      Can transform harmful shaping rewards into beneficial ones by learning\n      negative weights.
